					<h1 id="firstHeading" class="firstHeading mw-first-heading"><span class="mw-page-title-main">Politics</span></h1>
							
<div id="p-lang-btn" class="vector-dropdown mw-portlet mw-portlet-lang"  >
	<input type="checkbox" id="p-lang-btn-checkbox" role="button" aria-haspopup="true" data-event-name="ui.dropdown-p-lang-btn" class="vector-dropdown-checkbox mw-interlanguage-selector" aria-label="Go to an article in another language. Available in 208 languages"   >
	<label id="p-lang-btn-label" for="p-lang-btn-checkbox" class="vector-dropdown-label cdx-button cdx-button--fake-button cdx-button--fake-button--enabled cdx-button--weight-quiet cdx-button--action-progressive mw-portlet-lang-heading-208" aria-hidden="true"  ><span class="vector-icon mw-ui-icon-language-progressive mw-ui-icon-wikimedia-language-progressive"></span>

<span class="vector-dropdown-label-text">208 languages</span>
	</label>
	<div class="vector-dropdown-content">

		<div class="vector-menu-content">

<span class="nowrap"><span class="mw-image-border noviewer" typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:A_coloured_voting_box.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="icon" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/0/01/A_coloured_voting_box.svg/20px-A_coloured_voting_box.svg.png" decoding="async" width="15" height="15" class="mw-file-element" href="/wiki/Portal:Politics" title="Portal:Politics">Politics&#32;portal</a></td></tr><tr><td class="sidebar-navbar"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1333133064" /><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1239400231">.mw-parser-output .navbar{display:inline;font-size:88%;font-weight:normal}.mw-parser-output .navbar-collapse{float:left;text-align:left}.mw-parser-output .navbar-boxtext{word-spacing:0}.mw-parser-output .navbar ul{display:inline-block;white-space:nowrap;line-height:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-brackets::before{margin-right:-0.125em;content:"[ "}.mw-parser-output .navbar-brackets::after{margin-left:-0.125em;content:" ]"}.mw-parser-output .navbar li{word-spacing:-0.125em}.mw-parser-output .navbar a>span,.mw-parser-output .navbar a>abbr{text-decoration:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-mini abbr{font-variant:small-caps;border-bottom:none;text-decoration:none;cursor:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-ct-full{font-size:114%;margin:0 7em}.mw-parser-output .navbar-ct-mini{font-size:114%;margin:0 4em}html.skin-theme-clientpref-night .mw-parser-output .navbar li a abbr{color:var(--color-base)!important}@media(prefers-color-scheme:dark){html.skin-theme-clientpref-os .mw-parser-output .navbar li a abbr{color:var(--color-base)!important}}@media print{.mw-parser-output .navbar{display:none!important}}</style><div class="navbar plainlinks hlist navbar-mini"><ul><li class="nv-view"><a href="/wiki/Template:Politics_sidebar" title="Template:Politics sidebar"><abbr title="View this template">v</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-talk"><a href="/wiki/Template_talk:Politics_sidebar" title="Template talk:Politics sidebar"><abbr title="Discuss this template">t</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-edit"><a href="/wiki/Special:EditPage/Template:Politics_sidebar" title="Special:EditPage/Template:Politics sidebar"><abbr title="Edit this template">e</abbr></a></li></ul></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>Politics</b><sup id="cite&#95;ref-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-1"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>a<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> is the set of activities that are associated with <a href="/wiki/Decision-making" title="Decision-making">making decisions</a> in <a href="/wiki/Social_group" title="Social group">groups</a>, or other forms of <a href="/wiki/Power_(social_and_political)" title="Power (social and political)">power relations</a> among individuals, such as the distribution of <a href="/wiki/Social_status" title="Social status">status</a> or <a href="/wiki/Resource" title="Resource">resources</a>.
The branch of <a href="/wiki/Social_science" title="Social science">social science</a> that studies politics and <a href="/wiki/Government" title="Government">government</a> is referred to as <a href="/wiki/Political_science" title="Political science">political science</a>.
</p><p>Politics may be used positively in the context of a "political solution" which is compromising and non-violent,<sup id="cite&#95;ref-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-2"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>1<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> or descriptively as "the art or science of government", but the word often also carries a negative connotation.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-HagueHarrop2013&#95;3-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-HagueHarrop2013-3"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>2<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> The concept has been defined in various ways, and different approaches have fundamentally differing views on whether it should be used extensively or in a limited way, empirically or normatively, and on whether conflict or co-operation is more essential to it.
</p><p>A variety of methods are deployed in politics, which include <a href="/wiki/Political_campaign" title="Political campaign">promoting one's own political views</a> among people, <a href="/wiki/Negotiation" title="Negotiation">negotiation</a> with other political subjects, making <a href="/wiki/Law" title="Law">laws</a>, and exercising internal and external <a href="/wiki/Force_(law)" title="Force (law)">force</a>, including <a href="/wiki/Warfare" class="mw-redirect" title="Warfare">warfare</a> against adversaries.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Hammarlund1985&#95;4-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hammarlund1985-4"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>3<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-Brady2017&#95;5-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Brady2017-5"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>4<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-HawkesworthKogan2013&#95;6-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-HawkesworthKogan2013-6"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>5<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-Taylor2012&#95;7-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Taylor2012-7"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>6<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-8"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>7<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Politics is exercised on a wide range of social levels, from <a href="/wiki/Clans" class="mw-redirect" title="Clans">clans</a> and <a href="/wiki/Tribes" class="mw-redirect" title="Tribes">tribes</a> of traditional <a href="/wiki/Society" title="Society">societies</a>, through modern <a href="/wiki/Local_government" title="Local government">local governments</a>, <a href="/wiki/Company" title="Company">companies</a> and <a href="/wiki/Institution" title="Institution">institutions</a> up to <a href="/wiki/Sovereign_state" title="Sovereign state">sovereign states</a>, to the <a href="/wiki/International_politics" class="mw-redirect" title="International politics">international level</a>.
</p><p>In modern <a href="/wiki/State_(polity)" title="State (polity)">states</a>, people often form <a href="/wiki/Political_parties" class="mw-redirect" title="Political parties">political parties</a> to represent their ideas. Members of a party often agree to take the same position on many issues and agree to support the same changes to law and the same leaders. An <a href="/wiki/Election" title="Election">election</a> is usually a competition between different parties.
</p><p>A <a href="/wiki/Political_system" title="Political system">political system</a> is a framework which defines acceptable political methods within a society. The <a href="/wiki/History_of_political_thought" title="History of political thought">history of political thought</a> can be traced back to early antiquity, with seminal works such as <a href="/wiki/Plato" title="Plato">Plato</a>'s <i><a href="/wiki/Republic_(Plato)" title="Republic (Plato)">Republic</a></i>, <a href="/wiki/Aristotle" title="Aristotle">Aristotle</a>'s <i><a href="/wiki/Politics_(Aristotle)" title="Politics (Aristotle)">Politics</a></i>, <a href="/wiki/Confucius" title="Confucius">Confucius</a>'s political manuscripts and <a href="/wiki/Chanakya" title="Chanakya">Chanakya</a>'s <i><a href="/wiki/Arthashastra" title="Arthashastra">Arthashastra</a></i>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-9"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>8<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="Etymology">Etymology</h2><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Politics&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Etymology"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<p>The English word <i>politics</i> has its roots in the name of <a href="/wiki/Aristotle" title="Aristotle">Aristotle</a>'s classic work, <i><a href="/wiki/Politics_(Aristotle)" title="Politics (Aristotle)">Politiká</a></i>, which introduced the <a href="/wiki/Ancient_Greek" title="Ancient Greek">Ancient Greek</a> term <span title="Ancient Greek (to 1453)-language romanization"><i lang="grc-Latn"><i>politiká</i></i></span> (<span title="Ancient Greek-language text"><i lang="grc">Πολιτικά</i></span>, <span class="gloss-quot">'</span><span class="gloss-text">affairs of the cities</span><span class="gloss-quot">'</span>)<i>.</i> In the mid-15th century, Aristotle's composition was rendered in <a href="/wiki/Early_Modern_English" title="Early Modern English">Early Modern English</a> as <i>Polettiques</i>&#32;&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Sic" title="Sic">sic</a></i>&#93;,<sup id="cite&#95;ref-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-10"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>b<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-11"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>9<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> which became <i>Politics</i> in <a href="/wiki/Modern_English" title="Modern English">Modern English</a>.
</p><p>The singular <i>politic</i> first attested in English in 1430, coming from <a href="/wiki/Middle_French" title="Middle French">Middle French</a> <span title="French-language text"><i lang="fr">politique</i></span>—itself taking from <span title="Latin-language text"><i lang="la">politicus</i></span>,<sup id="cite&#95;ref-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-12"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>10<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> a <a href="/wiki/Latinisation_of_names" title="Latinisation of names">Latinization</a> of the Greek <span title="Ancient Greek-language text"><i lang="grc">πολιτικός</i></span> (<span title="Ancient Greek (to 1453)-language romanization"><i lang="grc-Latn">politikos</i></span>) from <span title="Ancient Greek-language text"><span lang="grc">πολίτης</span></span> (<span title="Ancient Greek-language text"><i lang="grc">polites</i></span>, <span class="gloss-quot">'</span><span class="gloss-text">citizen</span><span class="gloss-quot">'</span>) and <span title="Ancient Greek-language text"><span lang="grc">πόλις</span></span> (<span title="Ancient Greek-language text"><i lang="grc"><a href="/wiki/Polis" title="Polis">polis</a></i></span>, <span class="gloss-quot">'</span><span class="gloss-text">city</span><span class="gloss-quot">'</span>).<sup id="cite&#95;ref-A&#95;Greek-English&#95;Lexicon&#95;13-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-A_Greek-English_Lexicon-13"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>11<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Definitions">Definitions</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Politics&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Definitions"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Harold_Lasswell" title="Harold Lasswell">Harold Lasswell</a>: "who gets what, when, how"<sup id="cite&#95;ref-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-14"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>12<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/David_Easton" title="David Easton">David Easton</a>: "the authoritative allocation of values for a society"<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Easton&#95;1981&#95;15-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Easton_1981-15"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>13<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Vladimir_Lenin" title="Vladimir Lenin">Vladimir Lenin</a>: "the most concentrated expression of economics"<sup id="cite&#95;ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>14<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Otto_von_Bismarck" title="Otto von Bismarck">Otto von Bismarck</a>: "the capacity of always choosing at each instant, in constantly changing situations, the least harmful, the most useful"<sup id="cite&#95;ref-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-17"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>15<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Bernard_Crick" title="Bernard Crick">Bernard Crick</a>: "a distinctive form of rule whereby people act together through institutionalized procedures to resolve differences"<sup id="cite&#95;ref-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-18"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>16<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Adrian_Leftwich" title="Adrian Leftwich">Adrian Leftwich</a>: "comprises all the activities of co-operation, negotiation and conflict within and between societies"<sup id="cite&#95;ref-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-19"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>17<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup></li></ul>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="Approaches">Approaches</h2><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Politics&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Approaches"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<p>There are several ways in which approaching politics has been conceptualized.
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Extensive_and_limited">Extensive and limited</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Politics&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Extensive and limited"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<p><a href="/wiki/Adrian_Leftwich" title="Adrian Leftwich">Adrian Leftwich</a> has differentiated views of politics based on how extensive or limited their perception of what accounts as 'political' is.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-20"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>18<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> The extensive view sees politics as present across the sphere of human social relations, while the limited view restricts it to certain contexts. For example, in a more restrictive way, politics may be viewed as primarily about <a href="/wiki/Governance" title="Governance">governance</a>,<sup id="cite&#95;ref-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-21"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>19<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> while a <a href="/wiki/Feminist_political_theory" title="Feminist political theory">feminist perspective</a> could argue that sites which have been viewed traditionally as non-political, should indeed be viewed as political as well.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>20<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> This latter position is encapsulated in the slogan "<i><a href="/wiki/The_personal_is_political" title="The personal is political">the personal is political</a></i>", which disputes the distinction between private and public issues. Politics may also be defined by the use of power, as has been argued by <a href="/wiki/Robert_A._Dahl" class="mw-redirect" title="Robert A. Dahl">Robert A. Dahl</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>21<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Moralism_and_realism">Moralism and realism</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Politics&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Moralism and realism"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<p>Some perspectives on politics view it empirically as an exercise of power, while others see it as a social function with a <a href="/wiki/Normative" class="mw-redirect" title="Normative">normative</a> basis.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-24"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>22<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> This distinction has been called the difference between <a href="/wiki/Moralism" title="Moralism">political <i>moralism</i></a> and <a href="/wiki/Realism_(international_relations)" title="Realism (international relations)">political <i>realism</i></a><i>.</i><sup id="cite&#95;ref-:3&#95;25-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:3-25"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>23<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> For moralists, politics is closely linked to <a href="/wiki/Ethics" title="Ethics">ethics</a>, and is at its extreme in <a href="/wiki/Utopia" title="Utopia">utopian</a> thinking.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-:3&#95;25-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:3-25"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>23<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> For example, according to <a href="/wiki/Hannah_Arendt" title="Hannah Arendt">Hannah Arendt</a>, the view of <a href="/wiki/Aristotle" title="Aristotle">Aristotle</a> was that, "to be political…meant that everything was decided through words and persuasion and not through violence";<sup id="cite&#95;ref-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-26"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>24<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> while according to <a href="/wiki/Bernard_Crick" title="Bernard Crick">Bernard Crick</a>, "politics is the way in which free societies are governed. Politics is politics, and other forms of rule are something else."<sup id="cite&#95;ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>25<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> In contrast, for realists, represented by those such as <a href="/wiki/Niccol%C3%B2_Machiavelli" title="Niccolò Machiavelli">Niccolò Machiavelli</a>, <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Hobbes" title="Thomas Hobbes">Thomas Hobbes</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Harold_Lasswell" title="Harold Lasswell">Harold Lasswell</a>, politics is based on the use of power, irrespective of the ends being pursued.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>26<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-:3&#95;25-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:3-25"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>23<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Conflict_and_co-operation">Conflict and co-operation</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Politics&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Conflict and co-operation"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<p><a href="/wiki/Agonism" title="Agonism">Agonism</a> argues that politics essentially comes down to conflict between conflicting interests. Political scientist Elmer Schattschneider argued that "at the root of all politics is the universal language of conflict",<sup id="cite&#95;ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>27<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> while for <a href="/wiki/Carl_Schmitt" title="Carl Schmitt">Carl Schmitt</a> the essence of politics is the distinction of 'friend' from 'foe'.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>28<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> This is in direct contrast to the more co-operative views of politics by Aristotle and Crick. However, a more mixed view between these extremes is provided by Irish political scientist Michael Laver, who noted that:</p><blockquote><p>Politics is about the characteristic blend of conflict and co-operation that can be found so often in human interactions. Pure conflict is war. Pure co-operation is true love. Politics is a mixture of both.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>29<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup></p></blockquote>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="History">History</h2><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Politics&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: History"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Political_history_of_the_world" title="Political history of the world">Political history of the world</a></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/History_of_political_thought" title="History of political thought">History of political thought</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Head_of_Aristotle.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/05/Head_of_Aristotle.jpg/250px-Head_of_Aristotle.jpg" decoding="async" width="200" height="267" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/05/Head_of_Aristotle.jpg/330px-Head_of_Aristotle.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/05/Head_of_Aristotle.jpg/500px-Head_of_Aristotle.jpg 2x" data-file-width="636" data-file-height="850" /></a><figcaption>The Greek philosopher <a href="/wiki/Aristotle" title="Aristotle">Aristotle</a> criticized many of <a href="/wiki/Plato" title="Plato">Plato</a>'s ideas as impracticable, but, like Plato, he admires balance and moderation and aims at a harmonious city under the rule of law.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-32" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-32"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>30<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup></figcaption></figure>
<p>The history of politics encompasses the entirety of <a href="/wiki/Human_history" title="Human history">human history</a> and is not confined to the development of modern <a href="/wiki/Government" title="Government">governmental institutions</a> 
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Prehistoric">Prehistoric</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Politics&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Prehistoric"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<p>Early human forms of social organization—bands and tribes—lacked centralized political structures.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-33"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>31<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> These are sometimes referred to as <a href="/wiki/Stateless_society" title="Stateless society">stateless societies</a>.
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Early_states">Early states</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Politics&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Early states"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<p>In ancient history, <a href="/wiki/Ancient_civilizations" class="mw-redirect" title="Ancient civilizations">civilizations</a> did not have definite boundaries as <a href="/wiki/Nation_state" title="Nation state">states</a> have today, and their borders could be more accurately described as <a href="/wiki/Frontier" title="Frontier">frontiers</a>. <a href="/wiki/Sumer#Early_Dynastic_Period" title="Sumer">Early dynastic Sumer</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Ancient_Egypt#Early_Dynastic_Period" title="Ancient Egypt">early dynastic Egypt</a> were the <a href="/wiki/First_civilization" class="mw-redirect" title="First civilization">first civilizations</a> to define their <a href="/wiki/Border" title="Border">borders</a>. Moreover, up to the 12th century, many people lived in non-state societies. These range from relatively egalitarian <a href="/wiki/Band_society" title="Band society">bands</a> and <a href="/wiki/Tribe" title="Tribe">tribes</a> to complex and highly stratified <a href="/wiki/Chiefdom" title="Chiefdom">chiefdoms</a>.
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading4"><h4 id="State_formation">State formation</h4><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Politics&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: State formation"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/State_formation" title="State formation">State formation</a></div>
<p>There are a number of different theories and hypotheses regarding early state formation that seek generalizations to explain why <b><a href="/wiki/State_(polity)" title="State (polity)">the state</a></b> developed in some places but not others. Other scholars believe that generalizations are unhelpful and that each case of early state formation should be treated on its own.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>32<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p><b>Voluntary theories</b> contend that diverse groups of people came together to form states as a result of some shared rational interest.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Carneiro&#95;733–738&#95;35-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Carneiro_733–738-35"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>33<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> The theories largely focus on the development of agriculture, and the population and organizational pressure that followed and resulted in state formation. One of the most prominent theories of early and primary state formation is the <i>hydraulic hypothesis</i>, which contends that the state was a result of the need to build and maintain large-scale irrigation projects.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>34<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p><b><a href="/wiki/Conflict_theories" title="Conflict theories">Conflict theories</a></b> of state formation regard conflict and dominance of some population over another population as key to the formation of states.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Carneiro&#95;733–738&#95;35-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Carneiro_733–738-35"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>33<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> In contrast with voluntary theories, these arguments believe that people do not voluntarily agree to create a state to maximize benefits, but that states form due to some form of oppression by one group over others. Some theories in turn argue that warfare was critical for state formation.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Carneiro&#95;733–738&#95;35-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Carneiro_733–738-35"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>33<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading4"><h4 id="Ancient_history">Ancient history</h4><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Politics&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Ancient history"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<p>The first states of sorts were those of <a href="/wiki/Sumer#Early_Dynastic_Period" title="Sumer">early dynastic Sumer</a> and <a href="/wiki/Ancient_Egypt#Early_Dynastic_Period" title="Ancient Egypt">early dynastic Egypt</a>, which arose from the <a href="/wiki/Uruk_period" title="Uruk period">Uruk period</a> and <a href="/wiki/Predynastic_Egypt" class="mw-redirect" title="Predynastic Egypt">Predynastic Egypt</a> respectively around approximately 3000 BC.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-ancientlocations&#95;37-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ancientlocations-37"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>35<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Early dynastic Egypt was based around the <a href="/wiki/Nile_River" class="mw-redirect" title="Nile River">Nile River</a> in the north-east of <a href="/wiki/Africa" title="Africa">Africa</a>, the kingdom's boundaries being based around the Nile and stretching to areas where <a href="/wiki/Oasis" title="Oasis">oases</a> existed.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>36<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Early dynastic <a href="/wiki/Sumer" title="Sumer">Sumer</a> was located in southern <a href="/wiki/Mesopotamia" title="Mesopotamia">Mesopotamia</a>, with its borders extending from the <a href="/wiki/Persian_Gulf" title="Persian Gulf">Persian Gulf</a> to parts of the <a href="/wiki/Euphrates_River" class="mw-redirect" title="Euphrates River">Euphrates</a> and <a href="/wiki/Tigris_River" class="mw-redirect" title="Tigris River">Tigris</a> rivers.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-ancientlocations&#95;37-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ancientlocations-37"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>35<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>Egyptians, Romans, and the Greeks were the first people known to have explicitly formulated a political philosophy of the state, and to have rationally analyzed political institutions. Prior to this, states were described and justified in terms of religious myths.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>37<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>Several important political innovations of <a href="/wiki/Classical_antiquity" title="Classical antiquity">classical antiquity</a> came from the <a href="/wiki/Greek_city_states" class="mw-redirect" title="Greek city states">Greek city-states</a> (<i><a href="/wiki/Polis" title="Polis">polis</a></i>) and the <a href="/wiki/Ancient_Rome" title="Ancient Rome">Roman Republic</a>. The Greek city-states before the 4th century granted <a href="/wiki/Athenian_democracy#Citizenship_in_Athens" title="Athenian democracy">citizenship</a> rights to their free population; in <a href="/wiki/Athens" title="Athens">Athens</a> these rights <a href="/wiki/Athenian_democracy" title="Athenian democracy">were combined</a> with a <a href="/wiki/Direct_democracy" title="Direct democracy">directly democratic</a> form of government that was to have a long afterlife in political thought and history.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-40"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>38<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Modern_states">Modern states</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Politics&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Modern states"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Women_voter_outreach_1935_English_Yiddish.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3e/Women_voter_outreach_1935_English_Yiddish.jpg/250px-Women_voter_outreach_1935_English_Yiddish.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="199" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3e/Women_voter_outreach_1935_English_Yiddish.jpg/500px-Women_voter_outreach_1935_English_Yiddish.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="2860" data-file-height="2277" /></a><figcaption>Women voter outreach (1935)</figcaption></figure>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Peace_of_Westphalia" title="Peace of Westphalia">Peace of Westphalia</a> (1648) is considered by <a href="/wiki/Political_science" title="Political science">political scientists</a> to be the beginning of the modern international system,<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Osiander&#95;41-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Osiander-41"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>39<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-Gross&#95;42-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Gross-42"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>40<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>41<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> in which external powers should avoid interfering in another country's domestic affairs.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-kissinger&#95;world&#95;order&#95;44-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-kissinger_world_order-44"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>42<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> The principle of non-interference in other countries' domestic affairs was laid out in the mid-18th century by Swiss jurist <a href="/wiki/Emer_de_Vattel" title="Emer de Vattel">Emer de Vattel</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-krasner2010&#95;45-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-krasner2010-45"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>43<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> States became the primary institutional agents in an <a href="/wiki/Interstate_system_(world-systems_theory)" title="Interstate system (world-systems theory)">interstate system</a> of relations. The Peace of Westphalia is said to have ended attempts to impose supranational authority on European states. The "Westphalian" doctrine of states as independent agents was bolstered by the rise in 19th century thought of <a href="/wiki/Nationalism" title="Nationalism">nationalism</a>, under which legitimate <a href="/wiki/Sovereign_state" title="Sovereign state">states</a> were assumed to correspond to <i><a href="/wiki/Nations" class="mw-redirect" title="Nations">nations</a></i>—groups of people united by language and culture.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-46" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-46"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>44<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>In <a href="/wiki/Europe" title="Europe">Europe</a>, during the 18th century, the classic non-national states were the multinational <a href="/wiki/Empire" title="Empire">empires</a>: the <a href="/wiki/Austrian_Empire" title="Austrian Empire">Austrian Empire</a>, <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_France" title="Kingdom of France">Kingdom of France</a>, <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Hungary" title="Kingdom of Hungary">Kingdom of Hungary</a>,<sup id="cite&#95;ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>45<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> the <a href="/wiki/Russian_Empire" title="Russian Empire">Russian Empire</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Spanish_Empire" title="Spanish Empire">Spanish Empire</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Ottoman_Empire" title="Ottoman Empire">Ottoman Empire</a>, and the <a href="/wiki/British_Empire" title="British Empire">British Empire</a>. Such empires also existed in Asia, Africa, and the Americas; in the <a href="/wiki/Muslim_world" class="mw-redirect" title="Muslim world">Muslim world</a>, immediately after the <a href="/wiki/Death_of_Muhammad" class="mw-redirect" title="Death of Muhammad">death of Muhammad</a> in 632, <a href="/wiki/Caliphate" title="Caliphate">Caliphates</a> were established, which developed into multi-ethnic transnational empires.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-48"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>46<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> The multinational empire was an <a href="/wiki/Absolute_monarchy" title="Absolute monarchy">absolute monarchy</a> ruled by a king, <a href="/wiki/Emperor" title="Emperor">emperor</a> or <a href="/wiki/Sultan" title="Sultan">sultan</a>. The population belonged to many ethnic groups, and they spoke many languages. The empire was dominated by one ethnic group, and their language was usually the language of public administration. The ruling <a href="/wiki/Dynasty" title="Dynasty">dynasty</a> was usually, but not always, from that group. Some of the smaller European states were not so ethnically diverse, but were also <a href="/wiki/Dynasty" title="Dynasty">dynastic</a> states, ruled by a <a href="/wiki/Dynasty" title="Dynasty">royal house</a>. A few of the smaller states survived, such as the independent principalities of <a href="/wiki/Liechtenstein" title="Liechtenstein">Liechtenstein</a>, <a href="/wiki/Andorra" title="Andorra">Andorra</a>, <a href="/wiki/Monaco" title="Monaco">Monaco</a>, and the republic of <a href="/wiki/San_Marino" title="San Marino">San Marino</a>.
</p><p>Most theories see the nation state as a 19th-century European phenomenon, facilitated by developments such as state-mandated education, mass <a href="/wiki/Literacy" title="Literacy">literacy</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Mass_media" title="Mass media">mass media</a>. However, historians<sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Manual_of_Style/Words_to_watch#Unsupported_attributions" title="Wikipedia:Manual of Style/Words to watch"><span title="The material near this tag possibly uses too-vague attribution or weasel words. (August 2014)">who?</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> also note the early emergence of a relatively unified state and identity in <a href="/wiki/Portugal" title="Portugal">Portugal</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Dutch_Republic" title="Dutch Republic">Dutch Republic</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-49" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-49"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>47<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Scholars such as <a href="/wiki/Steven_Weber_(professor)" title="Steven Weber (professor)">Steven Weber</a>, <a href="/wiki/David_Woodward_(cartographer)" title="David Woodward (cartographer)">David Woodward</a>, <a href="/wiki/Michel_Foucault" title="Michel Foucault">Michel Foucault</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Jeremy_Black_(historian)" title="Jeremy Black (historian)">Jeremy Black</a> have advanced the hypothesis that the nation state did not arise out of political ingenuity or an unknown undetermined source, nor was it an accident of history or political invention.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-50" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-50"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>48<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-Carneiro&#95;733–738&#95;35-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Carneiro_733–738-35"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>33<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-51"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>49<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Rather, the nation state is an inadvertent byproduct of 15th-century intellectual discoveries in <a href="/wiki/Political_economy" title="Political economy">political economy</a>, <a href="/wiki/Capitalism" title="Capitalism">capitalism</a>, <a href="/wiki/Mercantilism" title="Mercantilism">mercantilism</a>, <a href="/wiki/Political_geography" title="Political geography">political geography</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Geography" title="Geography">geography</a><sup id="cite&#95;ref-52" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-52"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>50<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-53" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-53"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>51<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> combined with <a href="/wiki/Cartography" title="Cartography">cartography</a><sup id="cite&#95;ref-54" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-54"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>52<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-55" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-55"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>53<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> and <a href="/wiki/Automated_mapping" class="mw-redirect" title="Automated mapping">advances in map-making technologies</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-56" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-56"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>54<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>Some nation states, such as <a href="/wiki/Germany" title="Germany">Germany</a> and <a href="/wiki/Italy" title="Italy">Italy</a>, came into existence at least partly as a result of political campaigns by <a href="/wiki/Nationalism" title="Nationalism">nationalists</a>, during the 19th century. In both cases, the territory was previously divided among other states, some of them very small. Liberal ideas of <a href="/wiki/Free_trade" title="Free trade">free trade</a> played a role in German unification, which was preceded by a <a href="/wiki/Customs_union" title="Customs union">customs union</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Zollverein" title="Zollverein">Zollverein</a>. National <a href="/wiki/Self-determination" title="Self-determination">self-determination</a> was a key aspect of United States President <a href="/wiki/Woodrow_Wilson" title="Woodrow Wilson">Woodrow Wilson</a>'s <a href="/wiki/Fourteen_Points" title="Fourteen Points">Fourteen Points</a>, leading to the dissolution of the <a href="/wiki/Austro-Hungarian_Empire" class="mw-redirect" title="Austro-Hungarian Empire">Austro-Hungarian Empire</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Ottoman_Empire" title="Ottoman Empire">Ottoman Empire</a> after the <a href="/wiki/First_World_War" class="mw-redirect" title="First World War">First World War</a>, while the <a href="/wiki/Russian_Empire" title="Russian Empire">Russian Empire</a> became the <a href="/wiki/Soviet_Union" title="Soviet Union">Soviet Union</a> after the <a href="/wiki/Russian_Civil_War" title="Russian Civil War">Russian Civil War</a>. <a href="/wiki/Decolonization" title="Decolonization">Decolonization</a> lead to the creation of new nation states in place of multinational empires in the <a href="/wiki/Third_World" title="Third World">Third World</a>.
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Globalization">Globalization</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Politics&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: Globalization"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Political_globalization" title="Political globalization">Political globalization</a></div>
<p>Political globalization began in the 20th century through <a href="/wiki/Intergovernmental_organization" class="mw-redirect" title="Intergovernmental organization">intergovernmental organizations</a> and <a href="/wiki/Supranational_union" title="Supranational union">supranational unions</a>. The League of Nations was founded after <a href="/wiki/World_War_I" title="World War I">World War I</a>, and after <a href="/wiki/World_War_II" title="World War II">World War II</a> it was replaced by the <a href="/wiki/United_Nations" title="United Nations">United Nations</a>. Various <a href="/wiki/Treaty" title="Treaty">international treaties</a> have been signed through it. <a href="/wiki/Regional_integration" title="Regional integration">Regional integration</a> has been pursued by the <a href="/wiki/African_Union" title="African Union">African Union</a>, <a href="/wiki/ASEAN" title="ASEAN">ASEAN</a>, the <a href="/wiki/European_Union" title="European Union">European Union</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Mercosur" title="Mercosur">Mercosur</a>. International political institutions on the international level include the <a href="/wiki/International_Criminal_Court" title="International Criminal Court">International Criminal Court</a>, the <a href="/wiki/International_Monetary_Fund" title="International Monetary Fund">International Monetary Fund</a>, and the <a href="/wiki/World_Trade_Organization" title="World Trade Organization">World Trade Organization</a>.
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="Political_science">Political science</h2><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Politics&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: Political science"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1305433154">.mw-parser-output .ambox{border:1px solid #a2a9b1;border-left:10px solid #36c;background-color:#fbfbfb;box-sizing:border-box}.mw-parser-output .ambox+link+.ambox,.mw-parser-output .ambox+link+style+.ambox,.mw-parser-output .ambox+link+link+.ambox,.mw-parser-output .ambox+.mw-empty-elt+link+.ambox,.mw-parser-output .ambox+.mw-empty-elt+link+style+.ambox,.mw-parser-output .ambox+.mw-empty-elt+link+link+.ambox{margin-top:-1px}html body.mediawiki .mw-parser-output .ambox.mbox-small-left{margin:4px 1em 4px 0;overflow:hidden;width:238px;border-collapse:collapse;font-size:88%;line-height:1.25em}.mw-parser-output .ambox-speedy{border-left:10px solid #b32424;background-color:#fee7e6}.mw-parser-output .ambox-move{border-left:10px solid #9932cc}.mw-parser-output .ambox-protection{border-left:10px solid #a2a9b1}.mw-parser-output .ambox .mbox-text{border:none;padding:0.25em .ambox{margin:0 10%}}@media print{body.ns-0 .mw-parser-output .ambox{display:none!important}}</style><table class="box-More&#95;citations&#95;needed&#95;section plainlinks metadata ambox ambox-content ambox-Refimprove" role="presentation"><tbody><tr><td class="mbox-image"><div class="mbox-image-div"><span typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:Question_book-new.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img
