srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/9/99/Question_book-new.svg/120px-Question_book-new.svg.png 1.5x" data-file-width="512" data-file-height="399" /></a></span></div></td><td class="mbox-text"><div class="mbox-text-span">This section <b>needs additional citations for <a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Verifiability" title="Wikipedia:Verifiability">verification</a></b>.<span class="hide-when-compact"> Please help <a href="/wiki/Special:EditPage/Politics" title="Special:EditPage/Politics">improve this article</a> by <a href="/wiki/Help:Referencing_for_beginners" title="Help:Referencing for beginners">adding citations to reliable sources</a>&#32;in this section. Unsourced material may be challenged and removed.</span>  <span class="date-container"><i>(<span class="date">December 2020</span>)</i></span><span class="hide-when-compact"><i> (<small><a href="/wiki/Help:Maintenance_template_removal" title="Help:Maintenance template removal">Learn how and when to remove this message</a></small>)</i></span></div></td></tr></tbody></table><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Political_science" title="Political science">Political science</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Sanzio_01_Plato_Aristotle.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/98/Sanzio_01_Plato_Aristotle.jpg/250px-Sanzio_01_Plato_Aristotle.jpg" decoding="async" width="200" height="262" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/98/Sanzio_01_Plato_Aristotle.jpg/330px-Sanzio_01_Plato_Aristotle.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/98/Sanzio_01_Plato_Aristotle.jpg/500px-Sanzio_01_Plato_Aristotle.jpg 2x" data-file-width="804" data-file-height="1052" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Plato" title="Plato">Plato</a> (left) and <a href="/wiki/Aristotle" title="Aristotle">Aristotle</a> (right), from a detail of <i><a href="/wiki/The_School_of_Athens" title="The School of Athens">The School of Athens</a></i>, a fresco by <a href="/wiki/Raphael" title="Raphael">Raphael</a>. Plato's <i><a href="/wiki/Republic_(Plato)" title="Republic (Plato)">Republic</a></i> and Aristotle's <i><a href="/wiki/Politics_(Aristotle)" title="Politics (Aristotle)">Politics</a></i> secured the two Greek philosophers as two of the most influential political philosophers.</figcaption></figure>
<p>The study of politics is called <b>political science</b>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-57" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-57"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>55<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> It comprises numerous subfields, namely three: <a href="/wiki/Comparative_politics" title="Comparative politics">Comparative politics</a>, <a href="/wiki/International_relations" title="International relations">international relations</a> and <a href="/wiki/Political_theory" class="mw-redirect" title="Political theory">political philosophy</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-58" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-58"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>56<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Political science is related to, and draws upon, the fields of <a href="/wiki/Political_economy" title="Political economy">economics</a>, <a href="/wiki/Legal_education" title="Legal education">law</a>, <a href="/wiki/Political_sociology" title="Political sociology">sociology</a>, <a href="/wiki/Political_history" title="Political history">history</a>, <a href="/wiki/Political_philosophy" title="Political philosophy">philosophy</a>, <a href="/wiki/Political_geography" title="Political geography">geography</a>, <a href="/wiki/Political_psychology" title="Political psychology">psychology</a>, <a href="/wiki/Psychiatry" title="Psychiatry">psychiatry</a>, <a href="/wiki/Anthropology" title="Anthropology">anthropology</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Neuropolitics" title="Neuropolitics">neurosciences</a>.
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Comparative_politics" title="Comparative politics">Comparative politics</a> is the science of comparison and teaching of different types of <a href="/wiki/Constitutions" class="mw-redirect" title="Constitutions">constitutions</a>, political actors, legislature and associated fields. <a href="/wiki/International_relations" title="International relations">International relations</a> deals with the interaction between <a href="/wiki/Nation-state" class="mw-redirect" title="Nation-state">nation-states</a> as well as intergovernmental and transnational organizations. <a href="/wiki/Political_philosophy" title="Political philosophy">Political philosophy</a> is more concerned with contributions of various classical and contemporary thinkers and philosophers.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-59" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-59"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>57<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>Political science is methodologically diverse and appropriates many methods originating in <a href="/wiki/Psychology" title="Psychology">psychology</a>, <a href="/wiki/Social_research" title="Social research">social research</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Cognitive_neuroscience" title="Cognitive neuroscience">cognitive neuroscience</a>. Approaches include <a href="/wiki/Positivism" title="Positivism">positivism</a>, <a href="/wiki/Verstehen" title="Verstehen">interpretivism</a>, <a href="/wiki/Rational_choice_theory" class="mw-redirect" title="Rational choice theory">rational choice theory</a>, <a href="/wiki/Behavioralism" title="Behavioralism">behavioralism</a>, <a href="/wiki/Structuralism" title="Structuralism">structuralism</a>, <a href="/wiki/Post-structuralism" title="Post-structuralism">post-structuralism</a>, <a href="/wiki/Philosophical_realism" title="Philosophical realism">realism</a>, <a href="/wiki/Historical_institutionalism" title="Historical institutionalism">institutionalism</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Pluralism_(political_philosophy)" title="Pluralism (political philosophy)">pluralism</a>. Political science, as one of the <a href="/wiki/Social_science" title="Social science">social sciences</a>, uses methods and techniques that relate to the kinds of inquiries sought: primary sources such as historical documents and official records, secondary sources such as scholarly journal articles, <a href="/wiki/Sample_survey" class="mw-redirect" title="Sample survey">survey</a> research, <a href="/wiki/Statistics" title="Statistics">statistical analysis</a>, <a href="/wiki/Case_studies" class="mw-redirect" title="Case studies">case studies</a>, <a href="/wiki/Experimental_research" class="mw-redirect" title="Experimental research">experimental research</a>, and model building.
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="Political_system">Political system</h2><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Politics&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: Political system"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Political_system" title="Political system">Political system</a></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Systems_theory_in_political_science" title="Systems theory in political science">Systems theory in political science</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Map_of_European_nationals_coloured_by_percentage_of_vote_governing_party_got_in_last_election.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d0/Map_of_European_nationals_coloured_by_percentage_of_vote_governing_party_got_in_last_election.svg/250px-Map_of_European_nationals_coloured_by_percentage_of_vote_governing_party_got_in_last_election.svg.png" decoding="async" width="250" height="214" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d0/Map_of_European_nationals_coloured_by_percentage_of_vote_governing_party_got_in_last_election.svg/500px-Map_of_European_nationals_coloured_by_percentage_of_vote_governing_party_got_in_last_election.svg.png 1.5x" data-file-width="1062" data-file-height="907" /></a><figcaption>Map of European nations coloured by percentage of vote governing party got in last election as of 2022</figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Systems_view_of_politics.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/10/Systems_view_of_politics.png/250px-Systems_view_of_politics.png" decoding="async" width="250" height="139" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/10/Systems_view_of_politics.png/500px-Systems_view_of_politics.png 1.5x" data-file-width="796" data-file-height="441" /></a><figcaption>Systems view of politics</figcaption></figure><p>The political system defines the process for making official <a href="/wiki/Government" title="Government">government</a> decisions. It is usually compared to the <a href="/wiki/Legal_system" title="Legal system">legal system</a>, <a href="/wiki/Economic_system" title="Economic system">economic system</a>, <a href="/wiki/Cultural_system" title="Cultural system">cultural system</a>, and other <a href="/wiki/Social_system" title="Social system">social systems</a>. According to <a href="/wiki/David_Easton" title="David Easton">David Easton</a>, "A political system can be designated as the interactions through which values are authoritatively allocated for a society."<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Easton&#95;1981&#95;15-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Easton_1981-15"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>13<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Each political system is embedded in a society with its own political culture, and they in turn shape their societies through <a href="/wiki/Public_policy" title="Public policy">public policy</a>. The interactions between different political systems are the basis for <a href="/wiki/Global_politics" title="Global politics">global politics</a>.
</p><div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Forms_of_government">Forms of government</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Politics&amp;action=edit&amp;section=16" title="Edit section: Forms of government"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Eduskuntatalo_2_corrected_2020-03-23.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c3/Eduskuntatalo_2_corrected_2020-03-23.jpg/250px-Eduskuntatalo_2_corrected_2020-03-23.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="141" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c3/Eduskuntatalo_2_corrected_2020-03-23.jpg/500px-Eduskuntatalo_2_corrected_2020-03-23.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="3000" data-file-height="1688" /></a><figcaption>Legislatures are an important political institution. Pictured is the <a href="/wiki/Parliament_of_Finland" title="Parliament of Finland">Parliament of Finland</a>.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Forms of government can be classified by several ways. In terms of the <b>structure of power</b>, there are <a href="/wiki/Monarchy" title="Monarchy">monarchies</a> (including <a href="/wiki/Constitutional_monarchy" title="Constitutional monarchy">constitutional monarchies</a>) and <a href="/wiki/Republic" title="Republic">republics</a> (usually <a href="/wiki/Presidential_system" title="Presidential system">presidential</a>, <a href="/wiki/Semi-presidential_system" title="Semi-presidential system">semi-presidential</a>, or <a href="/wiki/Parliamentary_system" title="Parliamentary system">parliamentary</a>).
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Separation_of_powers" title="Separation of powers">separation of powers</a> describes the degree of <b>horizontal integration</b> between the <a href="/wiki/Legislature" title="Legislature">legislature</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Executive_(government)" title="Executive (government)">executive</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Judiciary" title="Judiciary">judiciary</a>, and other independent institutions.
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading4"><h4 id="Source_of_power">Source of power</h4><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Politics&amp;action=edit&amp;section=17" title="Edit section: Source of power"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<p>The source of power determines the difference between <a href="/wiki/Democracy" title="Democracy">democracies</a>, <a href="/wiki/Oligarchy" title="Oligarchy">oligarchies</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Autocracy" title="Autocracy">autocracies</a>.
</p><p>In a democracy, political <a href="/wiki/Legitimacy_(political)" class="mw-redirect" title="Legitimacy (political)">legitimacy</a> is based on <a href="/wiki/Popular_sovereignty" title="Popular sovereignty">popular sovereignty</a>. Forms of democracy include <a href="/wiki/Representative_democracy" title="Representative democracy">representative democracy</a>, <a href="/wiki/Direct_democracy" title="Direct democracy">direct democracy</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Sortition" title="Sortition">demarchy</a>. These are separated by the way decisions are made, whether by <a href="/wiki/Election" title="Election">elected</a> representatives, <a href="/wiki/Referendum" title="Referendum">referendums</a>, or by <a href="/wiki/Citizens%27_assembly" title="Citizens&#39; assembly">citizen juries</a>. Democracies can be either republics or constitutional monarchies.
</p><p>Oligarchy is a power structure where a minority rules. These may be in the form of <a href="/wiki/Anocracy" title="Anocracy">anocracy</a>, <a href="/wiki/Aristocracy" title="Aristocracy">aristocracy</a>, <a href="/wiki/Ergatocracy" class="mw-redirect" title="Ergatocracy">ergatocracy</a>, <a href="/wiki/Geniocracy" title="Geniocracy">geniocracy</a>, <a href="/wiki/Gerontocracy" title="Gerontocracy">gerontocracy</a>, <a href="/wiki/Kakistocracy" title="Kakistocracy">kakistocracy</a>, <a href="/wiki/Kleptocracy" title="Kleptocracy">kleptocracy</a>, <a href="/wiki/Meritocracy" title="Meritocracy">meritocracy</a>, noocracy, <a href="/wiki/Particracy" title="Particracy">particracy</a>, <a href="/wiki/Plutocracy" title="Plutocracy">plutocracy</a>, <a href="/wiki/Stratocracy" title="Stratocracy">stratocracy</a>, <a href="/wiki/Technocracy" title="Technocracy">technocracy</a>, <a href="/wiki/Theocracy" title="Theocracy">theocracy</a>, or <a href="/wiki/Timocracy" title="Timocracy">timocracy</a>.
</p><p>Autocracies are either <a href="/wiki/Dictatorship" title="Dictatorship">dictatorships</a> (including <a href="/wiki/Military_dictatorship" title="Military dictatorship">military dictatorships</a>) or <a href="/wiki/Absolute_monarchy" title="Absolute monarchy">absolute monarchies</a>.
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading4"><h4 id="Vertical_integration">Vertical integration</h4><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Politics&amp;action=edit&amp;section=18" title="Edit section: Vertical integration"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:The_pathway_of_regional_integration_or_separation.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/da/The_pathway_of_regional_integration_or_separation.png/250px-The_pathway_of_regional_integration_or_separation.png" decoding="async" width="250" height="69" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/da/The_pathway_of_regional_integration_or_separation.png/500px-The_pathway_of_regional_integration_or_separation.png 1.5x" data-file-width="977" data-file-height="268" /></a><figcaption>Relation between regional integration and <a href="/wiki/Separatism" title="Separatism">separatism</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>In terms of level of vertical integration, political systems can be divided into (from least to most integrated) <a href="/wiki/Confederation" title="Confederation">confederations</a>, <a href="/wiki/Federation" title="Federation">federations</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Unitary_state" title="Unitary state">unitary states</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-60" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-60"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>58<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>A federation (also known as a federal state) is a <a href="/wiki/Political_entity" class="mw-redirect" title="Political entity">political entity</a> characterized by a <a href="/wiki/Political_union" title="Political union">union</a> of partially <a href="/wiki/Federated_state" title="Federated state">self-governing provinces, states, or other regions</a> under a central <a href="/wiki/Federal_government" class="mw-redirect" title="Federal government">federal government</a> (<a href="/wiki/Federalism" title="Federalism">federalism</a>). In a federation, the self-governing status of the component states, as well as the division of power between them and the central government, is typically constitutionally entrenched and may not be altered by a unilateral decision of either party, the states or the federal political body. Federations were formed first in Switzerland, then in the United States in 1776, in Canada in 1867 and in Germany in 1871 and in 1901, <a href="/wiki/Australia" title="Australia">Australia</a>. Compared to a <a href="/wiki/Federation" title="Federation">federation</a>, a <a href="/wiki/Confederation" title="Confederation">confederation</a> has less centralized power.
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="State">State</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Politics&amp;action=edit&amp;section=19" title="Edit section: State"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Map_of_unitary_and_federal_states.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/46/Map_of_unitary_and_federal_states.svg/250px-Map_of_unitary_and_federal_states.svg.png" decoding="async" width="250" height="128" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/46/Map_of_unitary_and_federal_states.svg/500px-Map_of_unitary_and_federal_states.svg.png 1.5x" data-file-width="863" data-file-height="443" /></a><figcaption><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r981673959">.mw-parser-output .legend{page-break-inside:avoid;break-inside:avoid-column}.mw-parser-output .legend-color{display:inline-block;min-width:1.25em;height:1.25em;line-height:1.25;margin:1px 0;text-align:center;border:1px solid black;background-color:transparent;color:black}.mw-parser-output .legend-text{}</style><div class="legend"><span class="legend-color mw-no-invert" style="background-color:#00e000;; color:black;-webkit-print-color-adjust: exact; print-color-adjust: exact;">&#160;</span>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Federal_state" class="mw-redirect" title="Federal state">Federal states</a></div><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r981673959" /><div class="legend"><span class="legend-color mw-no-invert" style="background-color:#0000b0;; color:white;-webkit-print-color-adjust: exact; print-color-adjust: exact;">&#160;</span>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Unitary_state" title="Unitary state">Unitary states</a></div><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r981673959" /><div class="legend"><span class="legend-color mw-no-invert" style="background-color:#e1e1e1;; color:black;-webkit-print-color-adjust: exact; print-color-adjust: exact;">&#160;</span>&#160;No government</div></figcaption></figure>
<p>All the above forms of government are variations of the same basic <a href="/wiki/Polity" title="Polity">polity</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Sovereign_state" title="Sovereign state">sovereign state</a>. The <a href="/wiki/State_(polity)" title="State (polity)">state</a> has been defined by <a href="/wiki/Max_Weber" title="Max Weber">Max Weber</a> as a political entity that has <a href="/wiki/Monopoly_on_violence" title="Monopoly on violence">monopoly on violence</a> within its territory, while the <a href="/wiki/Montevideo_Convention" title="Montevideo Convention">Montevideo Convention</a> holds that states need to have a defined territory; a permanent population; a government; and a capacity to enter into international relations.
</p><p>A stateless society is a <a href="/wiki/Society" title="Society">society</a> that is not <a href="/wiki/Government" title="Government">governed</a> by a <a href="/wiki/State_(polity)" title="State (polity)">state</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Routledge2&#95;61-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Routledge2-61"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>59<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> In stateless societies, there is little <a href="/wiki/Centralization" class="mw-redirect" title="Centralization">concentration</a> of <a href="/wiki/Authority" title="Authority">authority</a>; most positions of authority that do exist are very limited in <a href="/wiki/Political_power" class="mw-redirect" title="Political power">power</a> and are generally not permanently held positions; and social bodies that resolve disputes through predefined rules tend to be small.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-62" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-62"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>60<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Stateless societies are highly variable in economic organization and cultural practices.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-63" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-63"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>61<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>While stateless societies were the norm in human prehistory, few stateless societies exist today; almost the entire global population resides within the jurisdiction of a <a href="/wiki/Sovereign_state" title="Sovereign state">sovereign state</a>. In some regions nominal state authorities may be very weak and wield <a href="/wiki/Failed_state" title="Failed state">little or no actual power</a>. Over the course of history most stateless peoples have been <a href="/wiki/Cultural_assimilation" title="Cultural assimilation">integrated into the state-based societies around them</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-64" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-64"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>62<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>Some political philosophies consider the state undesirable, and thus consider the formation of a stateless society a goal to be achieved. A central tenet of <a href="/wiki/Anarchism" title="Anarchism">anarchism</a> is the advocacy of society without states.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Routledge2&#95;61-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Routledge2-61"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>59<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-65" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-65"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>63<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> The type of society sought for varies significantly between <a href="/wiki/Anarchist_schools_of_thought" class="mw-redirect" title="Anarchist schools of thought">anarchist schools of thought</a>, ranging from extreme <a href="/wiki/Individualism" title="Individualism">individualism</a> to complete <a href="/wiki/Collectivism_and_individualism" class="mw-redirect" title="Collectivism and individualism">collectivism</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-slevin&#95;66-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-slevin-66"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>64<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> In <a href="/wiki/Marxism" title="Marxism">Marxism</a>, <a href="/wiki/Marx%27s_theory_of_the_state" title="Marx&#39;s theory of the state">Marx's theory of the state</a> considers that in a <a href="/wiki/Post-capitalist" class="mw-redirect" title="Post-capitalist">post-capitalist</a> society the state, an undesirable institution, would be unnecessary and <a href="/wiki/Withering_away_of_the_state" title="Withering away of the state">wither away</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-67" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-67"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>65<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> A related concept is that of <a href="/wiki/Stateless_communism" class="mw-redirect" title="Stateless communism">stateless communism</a>, a phrase sometimes used to describe Marx's anticipated post-capitalist society.
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Constitutions">Constitutions</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Politics&amp;action=edit&amp;section=20" title="Edit section: Constitutions"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<p><a href="/wiki/Constitutions" class="mw-redirect" title="Constitutions">Constitutions</a> are written documents that specify and limit the powers of the different branches of government. Although a constitution is a written document, there is also an unwritten constitution. The unwritten constitution is continually being written by the legislative and judiciary branch of government; this is just one of those cases in which the nature of the circumstances determines the form of government that is most appropriate.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-68" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-68"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>66<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> England did set the fashion of written constitutions during the <a href="/wiki/English_Civil_War" title="English Civil War">Civil War</a> but after the <a href="/wiki/Restoration_(England)" class="mw-redirect" title="Restoration (England)">Restoration</a> abandoned them to be taken up later by the <a href="/wiki/Thirteen_Colonies" title="Thirteen Colonies">American Colonies</a> after their <a href="/wiki/American_Revolution" title="American Revolution">emancipation</a> and then <a href="/wiki/France" title="France">France</a> after the <a href="/wiki/French_Revolution" title="French Revolution">Revolution</a> and the rest of Europe including the European colonies.
</p><p>Constitutions often set out <a href="/wiki/Separation_of_powers" title="Separation of powers">separation of powers</a>, dividing the government into the <a href="/wiki/Executive_(government)" title="Executive (government)">executive</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Legislature" title="Legislature">legislature</a>, and the <a href="/wiki/Judiciary" title="Judiciary">judiciary</a> (together referred to as the trias politica), in order to achieve checks and balances within the state. Additional independent branches may also be created, including <a href="/wiki/Civil_service_commission" title="Civil service commission">civil service commissions</a>, <a href="/wiki/Election_commission" title="Election commission">election commissions</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Supreme_audit_institution" title="Supreme audit institution">supreme audit institutions</a>.
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Political_culture">Political culture</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Politics&amp;action=edit&amp;section=21" title="Edit section: Political culture"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Inglehart-Values-Map-Small.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b2/Inglehart-Values-Map-Small.png/250px-Inglehart-Values-Map-Small.png" decoding="async" width="250" height="269" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/b/b2/Inglehart-Values-Map-Small.png 1.5x" data-file-width="400" data-file-height="431" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Inglehart%E2%80%93Welzel_cultural_map_of_the_world" title="Inglehart–Welzel cultural map of the world">Inglehart-Weltzel cultural map of countries</a></figcaption></figure>
<p><a href="/wiki/Political_culture" title="Political culture">Political culture</a> describes how <a href="/wiki/Culture" title="Culture">culture</a> impacts politics. Every <a href="/wiki/Political_system" title="Political system">political system</a> is embedded in a particular political culture.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-:13&#95;69-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:13-69"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>67<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Lucian_Pye" title="Lucian Pye">Lucian Pye</a>'s definition is that, "Political culture is the set of attitudes, beliefs, and sentiments, which give order and meaning to a political process and which provide the underlying assumptions and rules that govern behavior in the political system."<sup id="cite&#95;ref-:13&#95;69-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:13-69"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>67<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Trust_(social_science)" title="Trust (social science)">Trust</a> is a major factor in political culture, as its level determines the capacity of the state to function.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-:2&#95;70-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:2-70"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>68<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Postmaterialism" title="Postmaterialism">Postmaterialism</a> is the degree to which a political culture is concerned with issues which are not of immediate physical or material concern, such as <a href="/wiki/Human_rights" title="Human rights">human rights</a> and <a href="/wiki/Environmentalism" title="Environmentalism">environmentalism</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-:13&#95;69-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:13-69"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>67<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Religion" title="Religion">Religion</a> has also an impact on political culture.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-:2&#95;70-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:2-70"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>68<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Political_dysfunction">Political dysfunction</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Politics&amp;action=edit&amp;section=22" title="Edit section: Political dysfunction"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading4"><h4 id="Political_corruption">Political corruption</h4><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Politics&amp;action=edit&amp;section=23" title="Edit section: Political corruption"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Political_corruption" title="Political corruption">Political corruption</a></div>
<p>Political corruption is the use of powers for illegitimate private gain, conducted by government officials or their network contacts. Forms of political corruption include <a href="/wiki/Bribery" title="Bribery">bribery</a>, <a href="/wiki/Cronyism" title="Cronyism">cronyism</a>, <a href="/wiki/Nepotism" title="Nepotism">nepotism</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Patronage" title="Patronage">political patronage</a>. Forms of political patronage, in turn, includes <a href="/wiki/Clientelism" title="Clientelism">clientelism</a>, <a href="/wiki/Earmark_(politics)" title="Earmark (politics)">earmarking</a>, <a href="/wiki/Pork_barrel" title="Pork barrel">pork barreling</a>, <a href="/wiki/Slush_fund" title="Slush fund">slush funds</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Spoils_system" title="Spoils system">spoils systems</a>; as well as <a href="/wiki/Political_machine" title="Political machine">political machines</a>, which is a political system that operates for corrupt ends.
</p><p>When corruption is embedded in political culture, this may be referred to as <a href="/wiki/Patrimonialism" title="Patrimonialism">patrimonialism</a> or <a href="/wiki/Neopatrimonialism" title="Neopatrimonialism">neopatrimonialism</a>. A form of government that is built on corruption is called a <i><a href="/wiki/Kleptocracy" title="Kleptocracy">kleptocracy</a></i> ('rule of thieves').
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading4"><h4 id="Proxy_politics">Proxy politics</h4><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Politics&amp;action=edit&amp;section=24" title="Edit section: Proxy politics"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/Proxy_political_parties" class="mw-redirect" title="Proxy political parties">Proxy political parties</a> and <a href="/wiki/Proxy_wars" class="mw-redirect" title="Proxy wars">Proxy wars</a></div>
<p>The term "proxy politics" refers to specific forms of political influences and actions that are conducted through <a href="/wiki/Proxy_political_parties" class="mw-redirect" title="Proxy political parties">proxy political parties</a> (mainly in <a href="/wiki/Domestic_politics" class="mw-redirect" title="Domestic politics">domestic politics</a>), or through <a href="/wiki/Proxy_wars" class="mw-redirect" title="Proxy wars">proxy wars</a> (mainly in <a href="/wiki/International_politics" class="mw-redirect" title="International politics">international politics</a>).<sup id="cite&#95;ref-71" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-71"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>69<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading4"><h4 id="Insincere_politics">Insincere politics</h4><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Politics&amp;action=edit&amp;section=25" title="Edit section: Insincere politics"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<p>The words "politics" and "political" are sometimes used as pejoratives to mean political action that is deemed to be overzealous, performative, or insincere.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-72" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-72"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>70<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="Levels_of_politics">Levels of politics</h2><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Politics&amp;action=edit&amp;section=26" title="Edit section: Levels of politics"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Macropolitics">Macropolitics</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Politics&amp;action=edit&amp;section=27" title="Edit section: Macropolitics"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Global_politics" title="Global politics">Global politics</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:RIAN_archive_828797_Mikhail_Gorbachev_addressing_UN_General_Assembly_session.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/44/RIAN_archive_828797_Mikhail_Gorbachev_addressing_UN_General_Assembly_session.jpg/250px-RIAN_archive_828797_Mikhail_Gorbachev_addressing_UN_General_Assembly_session.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="170" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/44/RIAN_archive_828797_Mikhail_Gorbachev_addressing_UN_General_Assembly_session.jpg/500px-RIAN_archive_828797_Mikhail_Gorbachev_addressing_UN_General_Assembly_session.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="5221" data-file-height="3544" /></a><figcaption> <a href="/wiki/Mikhail_Gorbachev" title="Mikhail Gorbachev">Mikhail Gorbachev</a> addressing the UN General Assembly in 1988</figcaption></figure>
<p>Macropolitics can either describe political issues that affect an entire political system (e.g. the <a href="/wiki/Nation_state" title="Nation state">nation state</a>), or refer to interactions between political systems (e.g. <a href="/wiki/International_relations" title="International relations">international relations</a>).<sup id="cite&#95;ref-:0&#95;73-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-73"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>71<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>Global politics (or world politics) covers all aspects of politics that affect multiple political systems, in practice meaning any political phenomenon crossing national borders. This can include <a href="/wiki/City" title="City">cities</a>, nation-states, <a href="/wiki/Multinational_corporation" title="Multinational corporation">multinational corporations</a>, <a href="/wiki/Non-governmental_organization" title="Non-governmental organization">non-governmental organizations</a> or <a href="/wiki/International_organization" title="International organization">international organizations</a>. An important element is international relations: the relations between nation-states may be peaceful when they are conducted through <a href="/wiki/Diplomacy" title="Diplomacy">diplomacy</a>, or they may be violent, which is described as <a href="/wiki/War" title="War">war</a>. States that are able to exert strong international influence are referred to as <a href="/wiki/Superpower" title="Superpower">superpowers</a>, whereas less-powerful ones may be called <a href="/wiki/Regional_power" title="Regional power">regional</a> or <a href="/wiki/Middle_power" title="Middle power">middle powers</a>. The international system of <a href="/wiki/Power_(international_relations)" title="Power (international relations)">power</a> is called the <i>world order</i>, which is affected by the <a href="/wiki/Balance_of_power_(international_relations)" title="Balance of power (international relations)">balance of power</a> that defines the degree of <a href="/wiki/Polarity_(international_relations)" title="Polarity (international relations)">polarity</a> in the system. <a href="/wiki/Emerging_power" title="Emerging power">Emerging powers</a> are potentially destabilizing to it, especially if they display <a href="/wiki/Revanchism" title="Revanchism">revanchism</a> or <a href="/wiki/Irredentism" title="Irredentism">irredentism</a>.
</p><p>Politics inside the limits of political systems, which in contemporary context correspond to national <a href="/wiki/Border" title="Border">borders</a>, are referred to as <a href="/wiki/Domestic_politics" class="mw-redirect" title="Domestic politics">domestic politics</a>. This includes most forms of <a href="/wiki/Public_policy" title="Public policy">public policy</a>, such as <a href="/wiki/Social_policy" title="Social policy">social policy</a>, <a href="/wiki/Economic_policy" title="Economic policy">economic policy</a>, or <a href="/wiki/Law_enforcement" title="Law enforcement">law enforcement</a>, which are executed by the state <a href="/wiki/Bureaucracy" title="Bureaucracy">bureaucracy</a>.
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Mesopolitics"><span class="anchor" id="Mesopolitics"></span>Mesopolitics</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Politics&amp;action=edit&amp;section=28" title="Edit section: Mesopolitics"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Council_room_of_the_Province_of_Chieti_-_Meeting_of_the_Brothers_of_Italy.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/15/Council_room_of_the_Province_of_Chieti_-_Meeting_of_the_Brothers_of_Italy.jpg/250px-Council_room_of_the_Province_of_Chieti_-_Meeting_of_the_Brothers_of_Italy.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="188" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/15/Council_room_of_the_Province_of_Chieti_-_Meeting_of_the_Brothers_of_Italy.jpg/500px-Council_room_of_the_Province_of_Chieti_-_Meeting_of_the_Brothers_of_Italy.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="4000" data-file-height="3000" /></a><figcaption>Local political event organized by <a href="/wiki/Brothers_of_Italy" title="Brothers of Italy">Brothers of Italy</a> in the council room of the <a href="/wiki/Province_of_Chieti" title="Province of Chieti">Province of Chieti</a> in 2025</figcaption></figure>
<p>Mesopolitics describes the politics of intermediary structures within a political system, such as <a href="/wiki/Political_party" title="Political party">national political parties</a> or <a href="/wiki/Political_movement" title="Political movement">movements</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-:0&#95;73-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-73"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>71<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>A political party is a <a href="/wiki/Political_organization" class="mw-redirect" title="Political organization">political organization</a> that typically seeks to attain and maintain political power within <a href="/wiki/Government" title="Government">government</a>, usually by participating in <a href="/wiki/Political_campaign" title="Political campaign">political campaigns</a>, educational outreach, or <a href="/wiki/Protest_action" class="mw-redirect" title="Protest action">protest actions</a>. Parties often espouse an expressed <a href="/wiki/Ideology" title="Ideology">ideology</a> or vision, bolstered by a written <a href="/wiki/Party_platform" class="mw-redirect" title="Party platform">platform</a> with specific goals, forming a <a href="/wiki/Coalition" title="Coalition">coalition</a> among disparate interests.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-74" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-74"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>72<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>Political parties within a particular political system together form the <a href="/wiki/Party_system" title="Party system">party system</a>, which can be either <a href="/wiki/Multi-party_system" title="Multi-party system">multiparty</a>, <a href="/wiki/Two-party_system" title="Two-party system">two-party</a>, <a href="/wiki/Dominant-party_system" title="Dominant-party system">dominant-party</a>, or <a href="/wiki/One-party_state" title="One-party state">one-party</a>, depending on the level of <a href="/wiki/Pluralism_(political_theory)" title="Pluralism (political theory)">pluralism</a>. This is affected by characteristics of the political system, including its <a href="/wiki/Electoral_system" title="Electoral system">electoral system</a>. According to <a href="/wiki/Duverger%27s_law" title="Duverger&#39;s law">Duverger's law</a>, <a href="/wiki/First-past-the-post_voting" title="First-past-the-post voting">first-past-the-post</a> systems are likely to lead to two-party systems, while <a href="/wiki/Proportional_representation" title="Proportional representation">proportional representation</a> systems are more likely to create a multiparty system.
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Micropolitics"><span class="anchor" id="Micropolitics"></span>Micropolitics</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Politics&amp;action=edit&amp;section=29" title="Edit section: Micropolitics"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:(Pajero_Bishop_Scandal)_Filipinos_protest_against_the_Bishops_that_allegedly_accepted_money_and_luxury_cars_from_former_president_Macapagal-Arroyo_in_exchange_for_support.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/66/%28Pajero_Bishop_Scandal%29_Filipinos_protest_against_the_Bishops_that_allegedly_accepted_money_and_luxury_cars_from_former_president_Macapagal-Arroyo_in_exchange_for_support.jpg/250px-thumbnail.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="161" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/66/%28Pajero_Bishop_Scandal%29_Filipinos_protest_against_the_Bishops_that_allegedly_accepted_money_and_luxury_cars_from_former_president_Macapagal-Arroyo_in_exchange_for_support.jpg/500px-thumbnail.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="1024" data-file-height="660" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Philippines" title="Philippines">Filipino</a> protest for a local political controversy involving the Pajero <a href="/wiki/Bishop" title="Bishop">bishop</a> in 2011</figcaption></figure>
<p>Micropolitics describes the actions of individual actors within the political system.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-:0&#95;73-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-73"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>71<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> This is often described as <a href="/wiki/Political_participation" class="mw-redirect" title="Political participation">political participation</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-75" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-75"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>73<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Political participation may take many forms, including:
</p>
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Activism" title="Activism">Activism</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Boycott" title="Boycott">Boycott</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Civil_disobedience" title="Civil disobedience">Civil disobedience</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Demonstration_(political)" class="mw-redirect" title="Demonstration (political)">Demonstration</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Petition" title="Petition">Petition</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Picketing" title="Picketing">Picketing</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Strike_action" title="Strike action">Strike action</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Tax_resistance" title="Tax resistance">Tax resistance</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Voting" title="Voting">Voting</a> (or its opposite, <a href="/wiki/Abstentionism" title="Abstentionism">abstentionism</a>)</li></ul>

<span class="nowrap"><span class="mw-image-border noviewer" typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:A_coloured_voting_box.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="icon" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/0/01/A_coloured_voting_box.svg/20px-A_coloured_voting_box.svg.png" decoding="async" width="15" height="15" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/0/01/A_coloured_voting_box.svg/40px-A_coloured_voting_box.svg.png 1.5x" data-file-width="160" data-file-height="160" /></a></span> </span><a href="/wiki/Portal:Politics" title="Portal:Politics">Politics&#32;portal</a></td></tr><tr><td class="sidebar-navbar"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1333133064" /><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1239400231" /><div class="navbar plainlinks hlist navbar-mini"><ul><li class="nv-view"><a href="/wiki/Template:Democracy_sidebar" title="Template:Democracy sidebar"><abbr title="View this template">v</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-talk"><a href="/wiki/Template_talk:Democracy_sidebar" title="Template talk:Democracy sidebar"><abbr title="Discuss this template">t</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-edit"><a href="/wiki/Special:EditPage/Template:Democracy_sidebar" title="Special:EditPage/Template:Democracy sidebar"><abbr title="Edit this template">e</abbr></a></li></ul></div></td></tr></tbody></table><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Democracy" title="Democracy">Democracy</a></div>
<p>Democracy is a system of processing conflicts in which outcomes depend on what participants do, but no single force controls what occurs and its outcomes. The uncertainty of outcomes is inherent in democracy. Democracy makes all forces struggle repeatedly to realize their interests and devolves power from groups of people to sets of rules.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-76" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-76"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>74<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>Among modern political theorists, there are three contending conceptions of democracy: <i>aggregative</i>, <i><a href="/wiki/Deliberative_democracy" title="Deliberative democracy">deliberative</a></i>, and <i><a href="/wiki/Radical_democracy" title="Radical democracy">radical</a></i>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-77" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-77"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>75<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading4"><h4 id="Aggregation">Aggregation</h4><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Politics&amp;action=edit&amp;section=32" title="Edit section: Aggregation"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Aggregative_democracy" class="mw-redirect" title="Aggregative democracy">Aggregative democracy</a></div>
<p>The theory of <i>aggregative democracy</i> claims that the aim of the democratic processes is to solicit the preferences of citizens, and aggregate them together to determine what social policies the society should adopt. Therefore, proponents of this view hold that democratic participation should primarily focus on <a href="/wiki/Voting" title="Voting">voting</a>, where the policy with the most votes gets implemented.
</p><p>Different variants of aggregative democracy exist. Under <i>minimalism</i>, democracy is a system of government in which citizens have given teams of political leaders the right to rule in periodic elections. According to this minimalist conception, citizens cannot and should not "rule" because, for example, on most issues, most of the time, they have no clear views or their views are not well-founded. <a href="/wiki/Joseph_Schumpeter" title="Joseph Schumpeter">Joseph Schumpeter</a> articulated this view most famously in his book <i><a href="/wiki/Capitalism,_Socialism_and_Democracy" title="Capitalism, Socialism and Democracy">Capitalism, Socialism, and Democracy</a></i>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-78" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-78"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>76<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Contemporary proponents of minimalism include <a href="/wiki/William_H._Riker" title="William H. Riker">William H. Riker</a>, <a href="/wiki/Adam_Przeworski" title="Adam Przeworski">Adam Przeworski</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Richard_Posner" title="Richard Posner">Richard Posner</a>.
</p><p>According to the theory of <i><a href="/wiki/Direct_democracy" title="Direct democracy">direct democracy</a></i>, on the other hand, citizens should vote directly, not through their representatives, on legislative proposals. Proponents of direct democracy offer varied reasons to support this view. Political activity can be valuable in itself, it socialises and educates citizens, and popular participation can check powerful elites. Most importantly, citizens do not rule themselves unless they directly decide laws and policies.
</p><p>Governments will tend to produce laws and policies that are close to the views of the median voter—with half to their left and the other half to their right. This is not a desirable outcome as it represents the action of self-interested and somewhat unaccountable political elites competing for votes. <a href="/wiki/Anthony_Downs" title="Anthony Downs">Anthony Downs</a> suggests that ideological political parties are necessary to act as a mediating broker between individual and governments. Downs laid out this view in his 1957 book <i><a href="/wiki/An_Economic_Theory_of_Democracy" title="An Economic Theory of Democracy">An Economic Theory of Democracy</a></i>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-79" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-79"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>77<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading4"><h4 id="Polyarchy">Polyarchy</h4><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Politics&amp;action=edit&amp;section=33" title="Edit section: Polyarchy"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<p><a href="/wiki/Robert_A._Dahl" class="mw-redirect" title="Robert A. Dahl">Robert A. Dahl</a> argues that the fundamental democratic principle is that, when it comes to binding collective decisions, each person in a political community is entitled to have his/her interests be given equal consideration (not necessarily that all people are equally satisfied by the collective decision). He uses the term <a href="/wiki/Polyarchy" title="Polyarchy">polyarchy</a> to refer to societies in which there exists a certain set of institutions and procedures which are perceived as leading to such democracy. First and foremost among these institutions is the regular occurrence of free and open <a href="/wiki/Elections" class="mw-redirect" title="Elections">elections</a> which are used to select representatives who then manage all or most of the public policy of the society. However, these polyarchic procedures may not create a full democracy if, for example, poverty prevents political participation.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-80" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-80"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>78<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Similarly, <a href="/wiki/Ronald_Dworkin" title="Ronald Dworkin">Ronald Dworkin</a> argues that "democracy is a substantive, not a merely procedural, ideal".<sup id="cite&#95;ref-81" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-81"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>79<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading4"><h4 id="Deliberation">Deliberation</h4><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Politics&amp;action=edit&amp;section=34" title="Edit section: Deliberation"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<p><a href="/wiki/Deliberative_democracy" title="Deliberative democracy">Deliberative democracy</a> is based on the notion that democracy is government by <a href="/wiki/Deliberation" title="Deliberation">deliberation</a>. Unlike aggregative democracy, deliberative democracy holds that, for a democratic decision to be legitimate, it must be preceded by authentic deliberation, not merely the aggregation of preferences that occurs in voting. <i>Authentic deliberation</i> is deliberation among decision-makers that is free from distortions of unequal political power, such as power a decision-maker obtained through economic wealth or the support of interest groups.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-82" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-82"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>80<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-83" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-83"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>81<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-84" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-84"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>82<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> If the decision-makers cannot reach <a href="/wiki/Consensus_decision-making" title="Consensus decision-making">consensus</a> after authentically deliberating on a proposal, then they vote on the proposal using a form of majority rule.
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Equality">Equality</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Politics&amp;action=edit&amp;section=35" title="Edit section: Equality"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Social_equality" title="Social equality">Social equality</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Political_Compass_standard_model.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/64/Political_Compass_standard_model.svg/250px-Political_Compass_standard_model.svg.png" decoding="async" width="250" height="272" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/64/Political_Compass_standard_model.svg/500px-Political_Compass_standard_model.svg.png 1.5x" data-file-width="543" data-file-height="591" /></a><figcaption>Two-axis <a href="/wiki/Political_compass" class="mw-redirect" title="Political compass">political compass</a> chart with a horizontal socio-economic axis and a vertical socio-cultural axis and ideologically representative <a href="/wiki/Political_colour" title="Political colour">political colours</a>, an example for a frequently used model of the political spectrum<sup id="cite&#95;ref-:02&#95;85-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:02-85"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>83<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-:32&#95;86-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:32-86"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>84<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-87" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-87"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>85<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-:62&#95;88-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:62-88"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>86<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-89" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-89"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>87<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-:42&#95;90-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:42-90"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>88<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-91" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-91"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>89<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-92" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-92"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>90<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup></figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:3-axis-model-of-political-ideologies-with-both-moderate-and-radical-versions-and-policies-goals.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/93/3-axis-model-of-political-ideologies-with-both-moderate-and-radical-versions-and-policies-goals.png/250px-3-axis-model-of-political-ideologies-with-both-moderate-and-radical-versions-and-policies-goals.png" decoding="async" width="250" height="250" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/93/3-axis-model-of-political-ideologies-with-both-moderate-and-radical-versions-and-policies-goals.png/500px-3-axis-model-of-political-ideologies-with-both-moderate-and-radical-versions-and-policies-goals.png 1.5x" data-file-width="716" data-file-height="716" /></a><figcaption>Three axis model of political ideologies with both moderate and radical versions and the goals of their policies</figcaption></figure>
<p>Equality is a state of affairs in which all people within a specific <a href="/wiki/Society" title="Society">society</a> or isolated group have the same <a href="/wiki/Social_status" title="Social status">social status</a>, especially <a href="/wiki/Socioeconomic_status" title="Socioeconomic status">socioeconomic status</a>, including protection of <a href="/wiki/Human_rights" title="Human rights">human rights</a> and <a href="/wiki/Dignity" title="Dignity">dignity</a>, as well as access to certain <a href="/wiki/Social_goods" class="mw-redirect" title="Social goods">social goods</a> and <a href="/wiki/Social_services" title="Social services">social services</a>. Furthermore, it may also include <a href="/wiki/Health_equality" class="mw-redirect" title="Health equality">health equality</a>, <a href="/wiki/Equality_(economics)" class="mw-redirect" title="Equality (economics)">economic equality</a> and other <a href="/wiki/Social_security" class="mw-redirect" title="Social security">social securities</a>. Social equality requires the absence of legally enforced <a href="/wiki/Social_class" title="Social class">social class</a> or <a href="/wiki/Caste" title="Caste">caste</a> boundaries and the absence of <a href="/wiki/Discrimination" title="Discrimination">discrimination</a> based on by an inalienable aspect of a person's identity. To this end, there must be <a href="/wiki/Equal_justice_under_law" title="Equal justice under law">equal justice under law</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Equal_opportunity" title="Equal opportunity">equal opportunity</a> regardless of,  sex, gender, ethnicity, age, sexual orientation, origin, <a href="/wiki/Caste" title="Caste">caste</a> or class, income or property, language, <a href="/wiki/Religion" title="Religion">religion</a>, convictions, opinions, health or disability.
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading4"><h4 id="Left–right_spectrum"><span id="Left.E2.80.93right_spectrum"></span>Left–right spectrum</h4><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Politics&amp;action=edit&amp;section=36" title="Edit section: Left–right spectrum"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Left%E2%80%93right_political_spectrum" title="Left–right political spectrum">Left–right political spectrum</a></div>
<p>A common way of understanding politics is through the <a href="/wiki/Left%E2%80%93right_political_spectrum" title="Left–right political spectrum">left–right political spectrum</a>, which ranges from <a href="/wiki/Left-wing_politics" title="Left-wing politics">left-wing politics</a> via <a href="/wiki/Centrism" title="Centrism">centrism</a> to <a href="/wiki/Right-wing_politics" title="Right-wing politics">right-wing politics</a>. This classification is comparatively recent and dates from the <a href="/wiki/French_Revolution" title="French Revolution">French Revolution</a>, when those members of the <a href="/wiki/National_Assembly" title="National Assembly">National Assembly</a> who supported the <a href="/wiki/Republic" title="Republic">republic</a>, the common people and a <a href="/wiki/Secular_society" class="mw-redirect" title="Secular society">secular society</a> sat on the left and supporters of the <a href="/wiki/Monarchy" title="Monarchy">monarchy</a>, <a href="/wiki/Aristocracy_(class)" title="Aristocracy (class)">aristocratic</a> privilege and the Church sat on the right.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Knapp&#95;93-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Knapp-93"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>91<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>Today, the left is generally <a href="/wiki/Progressivism" title="Progressivism">progressivist</a>, seeking social <a href="/wiki/Progress" title="Progress">progress</a> in <a href="/wiki/Society" title="Society">society</a>. The more extreme elements of the left, named the <a href="/wiki/Far-left_politics" title="Far-left politics">far-left</a>, tend to support <a href="/wiki/Revolution" title="Revolution">revolutionary</a> means for achieving this. This includes ideologies such as <a href="/wiki/Communism" title="Communism">Communism</a> and <a href="/wiki/Marxism" title="Marxism">Marxism</a>. The <a href="/wiki/Centre-left_politics" title="Centre-left politics">center-left</a>, on the other hand, advocates for more <a href="/wiki/Reformism" title="Reformism">reformist</a> approaches, for example that of <a href="/wiki/Social_democracy" title="Social democracy">social democracy</a>.
</p><p>In contrast, the right is generally motivated by <a href="/wiki/Conservatism" title="Conservatism">conservatism</a>, which seeks to conserve what it sees as the important elements of society such as law and order, limited government and preserving individual freedoms. The <a href="/wiki/Far-right_politics" title="Far-right politics">far-right</a> goes beyond this, and often represents a <a href="/wiki/Reactionary" title="Reactionary">reactionary</a> turn against progress, seeking to undo it. Examples of such ideologies have included <a href="/wiki/Fascism" title="Fascism">Fascism</a> and <a href="/wiki/Nazism" title="Nazism">Nazism</a>. The <a href="/wiki/Centre-right_politics" title="Centre-right politics">center-right</a> may be less clear-cut and more mixed in this regard, with <a href="/wiki/Neoconservatism" title="Neoconservatism">neoconservatives</a> supporting the spread of <a href="/wiki/Free_market" title="Free market">free markets</a> and <a href="/wiki/Capitalism" title="Capitalism">capitalism</a>, and <a href="/wiki/One-nation_conservatism" title="One-nation conservatism">one-nation conservatives</a> more open to social welfare programs.
</p><p>According to <a href="/wiki/Norberto_Bobbio" title="Norberto Bobbio">Norberto Bobbio</a>, one of the major exponents of this distinction, the left believes in attempting to eradicate social inequality—believing it to be unethical or unnatural,<sup id="cite&#95;ref-94" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-94"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>92<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> while the right regards most social inequality as the result of ineradicable natural inequalities, and sees attempts to enforce social equality as utopian or authoritarian.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Bobbio&#95;95-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Bobbio-95"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>93<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
Some ideologies, notably <a href="/wiki/Christian_Democracy" class="mw-redirect" title="Christian Democracy">Christian Democracy</a>, claim to combine left and right-wing politics; according to Geoffrey K. Roberts and Patricia Hogwood, "In terms of ideology, Christian Democracy has incorporated many of the views held by liberals, conservatives and socialists within a wider framework of moral and Christian principles."<sup id="cite&#95;ref-96" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-96"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>94<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Movements which claim or formerly claimed to be above the left-right divide include Fascist <a href="/wiki/Terza_Posizione" title="Terza Posizione">Terza Posizione</a> economic politics in Italy and <a href="/wiki/Peronism" title="Peronism">Peronism</a> in Argentina.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-97" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-97"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>95<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-98" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-98"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>96<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Freedom">Freedom</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Politics&amp;action=edit&amp;section=37" title="Edit section: Freedom"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Political_freedom" title="Political freedom">Political freedom</a></div>
<p>Political freedom (also known as <b>political liberty</b> or <b>autonomy</b>) is a central <a href="/wiki/Concept" title="Concept">concept</a> in political thought and one of the most important features of <a href="/wiki/Democracy" title="Democracy">democratic</a> societies. <a href="/wiki/Negative_liberty" title="Negative liberty">Negative liberty</a> has been described as freedom from oppression or coercion and unreasonable external constraints on action, often enacted through <a href="/wiki/Civil_and_political_rights" title="Civil and political rights">civil and political rights</a>, while <a href="/wiki/Positive_liberty" title="Positive liberty">positive liberty</a> is the absence of disabling conditions for an individual and the fulfillment of enabling conditions, e.g. economic compulsion, in a society. This <a href="/wiki/Capability_approach" title="Capability approach">capability approach</a> to freedom requires <a href="/wiki/Economic,_social_and_cultural_rights" title="Economic, social and cultural rights">economic, social and cultural rights</a> in order to be realized.
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading4"><h4 id="Authoritarianism_and_libertarianism">Authoritarianism and libertarianism</h4><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Politics&amp;action=edit&amp;section=38" title="Edit section: Authoritarianism and libertarianism"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<p><a href="/wiki/Authoritarianism" title="Authoritarianism">Authoritarianism</a> and <a href="/wiki/Civil_libertarianism" title="Civil libertarianism">libertarianism</a> disagree the amount of individual <a href="/wiki/Freedom_(political)" class="mw-redirect" title="Freedom (political)">freedom</a> each person possesses in that society relative to the state. One author describes authoritarian political systems as those where "individual <a href="/wiki/Rights" title="Rights">rights</a> and goals are subjugated to group goals, expectations and conformities",<sup id="cite&#95;ref-99" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-99"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>97<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> while libertarians generally oppose the <a href="/wiki/Sovereign_state" title="Sovereign state">state</a> and hold the <a href="/wiki/Individual" title="Individual">individual</a> as sovereign. In their purest form, libertarians are <a href="/wiki/Anarchism" title="Anarchism">anarchists</a>,<sup id="cite&#95;ref-100" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-100"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>98<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> who argue for the total abolition of the state, of <a href="/wiki/Political_parties" class="mw-redirect" title="Political parties">political parties</a> and of <a href="/wiki/Political_organisation" title="Political organisation">other political entities</a>, while the purest authoritarians are, by definition, <a href="/wiki/Totalitarianism" title="Totalitarianism">totalitarians</a> who support state control over all aspects of society.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-101" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-101"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>99<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>For instance, <a href="/wiki/Classical_liberalism" title="Classical liberalism">classical liberalism</a> (also known as <i><a href="/wiki/Laissez-faire" title="Laissez-faire">laissez-faire</a> liberalism</i>)<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Adams&#95;102-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Adams-102"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>100<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> is a doctrine stressing individual freedom and <a href="/wiki/Limited_government" title="Limited government">limited government</a>. This includes the importance of human rationality, individual <a href="/wiki/Property_rights" class="mw-redirect" title="Property rights">property rights</a>, <a href="/wiki/Free_market" title="Free market">free markets</a>, <a href="/wiki/Natural_rights" class="mw-redirect" title="Natural rights">natural rights</a>, the protection of <a href="/wiki/Civil_liberties" title="Civil liberties">civil liberties</a>, constitutional limitation of government, and individual freedom from restraint as exemplified in the writings of <a href="/wiki/John_Locke" title="John Locke">John Locke</a>, <a href="/wiki/Adam_Smith" title="Adam Smith">Adam Smith</a>, <a href="/wiki/David_Hume" title="David Hume">David Hume</a>, <a href="/wiki/David_Ricardo" title="David Ricardo">David Ricardo</a>, <a href="/wiki/Voltaire" title="Voltaire">Voltaire</a>, <a href="/wiki/Montesquieu" title="Montesquieu">Montesquieu</a> and others. According to the libertarian <a href="/wiki/Institute_for_Humane_Studies" title="Institute for Humane Studies">Institute for Humane Studies</a>, "the libertarian, or 'classical liberal', perspective is that individual well-being, prosperity, and social harmony are fostered by 'as much liberty as possible' and 'as little government as necessary'."<sup id="cite&#95;ref-103" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-103"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>101<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> For anarchist political philosopher <a href="/wiki/L._Susan_Brown" class="mw-redirect" title="L. Susan Brown">L. Susan Brown</a> (1993), "liberalism and <a href="/wiki/Anarchism" title="Anarchism">anarchism</a> are two political philosophies that are fundamentally concerned with individual <a href="/wiki/Freedom_of_will" class="mw-redirect" title="Freedom of will">freedom</a> yet differ from one another in very distinct ways. Anarchism shares with liberalism a radical commitment to individual freedom while rejecting liberalism's competitive property relations."<sup id="cite&#95;ref-ReferenceA&#95;104-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceA-104"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>102<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>

<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Citations">Citations</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Politics&amp;action=edit&amp;section=42" title="Edit section: Citations"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1327269900" /><div class="mw-references-wrap mw-references-columns" style="column-width: calc( 0.9 * 30em );"><ol class="references">
<li id="cite&#95;note-2"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-2">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFLeftwich2015">Leftwich 2015</a>, p.&#160;68.</span>
</li>

<li id="cite&#95;note-47"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-47">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Eric Hobsbawm, <i>Nations and Nationalism since 1780: programme, myth, reality</i> (Cambridge Univ. Press, 1990; <link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1333433106" /><a href="/wiki/ISBN_(identifier)" class="mw-redirect" title="ISBN (identifier)">ISBN</a>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Special:BookSources/0-521-43961-2" title="Special:BookSources/0-521-43961-2"><bdi>0-521-43961-2</bdi></a>) chapter II "The popular protonationalism", pp. 80–81 French edition (Gallimard, 1992). According to Hobsbawm, the main source for this subject is Ferdinand Brunot (ed.), <i>Histoire de la langue française</i>, Paris, 1927–1943, 13 volumes, in particular volume IX. He also refers to Michel de Certeau, Dominique Julia, Judith Revel, Une politique de la langue: la Révolution française et les patois: l'enquête de l'abbé Grégoire, Paris, 1975. For the problem of the transformation of a minority official language into a widespread national language during and after the French Revolution, see Renée Balibar, L'Institution du français: essai sur le co-linguisme des Carolingiens à la République, Paris, 1985 (also Le co-linguisme, PUF, Que sais-je?, 1994, but out of print) <i>The Institution of the French language: essay on colinguism from the Carolingian to the Republic</i>. Finally, Hobsbawm refers to Renée Balibar and Dominique Laporte, Le Français national: politique et pratique de la langue nationale sous la Révolution, Paris, 1974.</span>

<li id="cite&#95;note-67"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-67">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1333433106" /><cite id="CITEREFEngels1880" class="citation book cs1">Engels, Frederick (1880). "Part III: Historical Materialism". <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.marxists.org/archive/marx/works/1880/soc-utop/index.htm"><i>Socialism: Utopian and Scientific</i></a>. <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://web.archive.org/web/20210207174438/https://www.marxists.org/archive/marx/works/1880/soc-utop/index.htm">Archived</a> from the original on 7 February 2021<span class="reference-accessdate">. Retrieved <span class="nowrap">4 May</span> 2020</span> &#8211; via Marx/Engels Internet Archive (marxists.org). <q>State interference in social relations becomes, in one domain after another, superfluous, and then dies out of itself; the government of persons is replaced by the administration of things, and by the conduct of processes of production. The State is not "abolished". It dies out...Socialized production upon a predetermined plan becomes henceforth possible. The development of production makes the existence of different classes of society thenceforth an anachronism. In proportion as anarchy in social production vanishes, the political authority of the State dies out. Man, at last the master of his own form of social organization, becomes at the same time the lord over Nature, his own master—free.</q></cite><span title="ctx&#95;ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft&#95;val&#95;fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Abook&amp;rft.genre=bookitem&amp;rft.atitle=Part+III%3A+Historical+Materialism&amp;rft.btitle=Socialism%3A+Utopian+and+Scientific&amp;rft.date=1880&amp;rft.aulast=Engels&amp;rft.aufirst=Frederick&amp;rft&#95;id=http%3A%2F%2Fwww.marxists.org%2Farchive%2Fmarx%2Fworks%2F1880%2Fsoc-utop%2Findex.htm&amp;rfr&#95;id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3APolitics" class="Z3988"></span></span>
